S.O,  2452. — ■Whereas  by  notification  of  the  Government 
of  India  it)  the  Ministry  of  Petroleum  and  Natural  Gas  S.O. 
No.  782  dated  27-2-90  under  sub-.seciion  (1)  of  Section  3  of 
the  Peroleum  and  Minerals  Pipelines  (Acquisition  of  Right 
of  User  in  Land)  Act,  1962  (50  ol  1962),  the  Central 
Government  declared  its  intention  to  acquire  the  right  of 
user  in  lands  specified  in  the  schedule  appended  to  that  noti¬ 
fication  for  the  purpose  of  laying  pijteline. 

And  whereas  the-  Competent  Authority  lias  under  sub¬ 
section  (1)  of  Section  6  of  the  said  Acl,  submitted  report 
to  the  Government  ; 

,\nd  further  whereas  the  Central  Government  has,  after 
considering  the  said  report  decided  to  acquire  the  rijfht  of 
user  in  the  lands  in  the  schedule  appended  to  tins  noti- 
lication  ; 

Now,  therefore,  in  exercise  of  the  power  conferred  by  sub- 
sectiojr  (1)  of  Section  6  of  the  said  Act,  the  Central 
Government  hereby  declaro.s  that  the  right  of  user  in  the 
said  lands'  specified  in  the  schedule  ‘appended  to  this  noti¬ 
fication  hereby  acquired  for  laying  the  pipeline  : 

And  further  in  exercise  of  power  conferred  by  sub-section 
(4)  of  the  section,  the  Central  Government  directs  that  the 
rights  of  user  in  the  said  lands  shall  instead  of  vesting  in 
Central  Government  vests  on  this  date  of  the  publication  of 
this  declaration  in  the  OH  and  Natural  Gas  Commission  free 
encumbrances.

S.O.  2454, — ^In  exercise  of  the  powers  conferred  by  sub- 
rule  (2)  of  rule  9,  clause  (b)  of  sub-rule  (2)  of  rule  12 
and  sub-rule  (1)  of  rule  24  of  Central  Civil  Services  Classifi¬ 
cation,  Control  and  Appeal)  Rules,  1965  and  in  supersession 
of  the  notifications  of  Government  of  India  in  the  Ministry 
of  Communications  (Department  of  Posts)  No.  S.O.  1587, 
dated  the  17th  June,  1989,  published  in  Gazette  of  India, 
Part  TI,  Section  3,  Sub-section  (ii),  dated  the  8th  July;  1989: 
the  Preaident  hereby  orders  that, — 


1.  In  respect  of  the  posts  in  General  Central  Servces  Group 
‘B’  specified  in  column  1  of  Part  1  of  the  Schedule 
to  this  Order,  the  authority  specified  in  column  2, 
shall  be  the  appointing  authority  and  the  authority 
specified  in  column  3  shall  be  the  disciplinary 
authority  in  regard  to  the  penalties  specified  in 
column  4; 


2,  in  respect  of  the  posts  in  General  (Central  Services 
Group  ‘C’  and  Group  ‘D’  specified  in  column  1  of 
Part  II  of  the  said  Schedule,  the  anthoritv  specified 
In  column  2  shall  be  the  appointing  authority  and 
the  authorities  specified  in  columns  3  and  5  shall  be 
the  disciplinarv  authority  and  appellate  nuthoritv 
respectively  in  regard  to  the  penalties  specified  in 
column  4,

the  rcciuired  study.  As  request  for  permitting  time 
for  e.tplanation  as  well  as  adjournment  of  enquiry 
proceedings  vvere  adequately  acceded  to  and  in  order 
that  the  enquiry  is  not  delayed  any  further,  which 
would  amount  to  delaying  justice  I  am  unable  to 
grant  this  request." 

5.  Thereupon  Claikward  told  the  Officer  that  he  had  nothing 
further  to  .‘■ay  and  the  latter  closed  the  inquiry.  He  sub¬ 
mitted  his  report  dated  17-7-87  (Ex,  M-8)  to  the  Senior  Jas- 
tallation  Manager.  By  his  order  dated  31-8-87  (Ex.  M-9), 
the  General  Manager  (Supplies  and  Distribution)  dismissed 
the  Workman,  holding  both  charges  duly  proved. 

6.  The  workman  .states  that  the  departmental  inquiry  stands 
vitiated  on  account  of  the  Enquiry  Officer’s  declining  his 
rcqiie.sls  for  Lawyer  and  for  time  to  study  the  documents 
and  prepare  his  difference.  According  to  him  this  has  resulted 
in  gross  piejudicc  to  him  on  merits,  The  Management  by 
their  written  statement  deny  that  any  prejudice  has  been 
caused  to  the  Workman  on  merits.  They  point  out  that 
Gnikwad's  services  were  available  to  the  Workman  and  in 
fact  the  former  did  effectively  represent  the  Workman  on 
all  material  date.s.  They  further  state  that  the  Enquiry  Officer 
was  right  in  declining  the  adjournment  on  the  last  date. 
They  have  kept  their  option  open  to  adduce  evidence  before 
the  Tribunal,  in  case  the  enquiry  is  held  to  he  defective 
for  any  I’eason. 

7.  1  shall  111  St  take  up  the  question  whether  the  Enquiry 
Officer  should  have  grunted  the  reque.st  of  the  Workman 
to  be  icpresented  .by  a  Lawyer  and  whether  rejection  of  this 
request  has  prejudicially  affected  the  Workman's  defence. 
Before  me  neither  piuty  ha.s  led  oral  evidence.  Documents 
filed  by  them  have  been  exhibited  by  consent.  1  have  also 
heard  the  Woikman  in  person  and  Shri  Rele  for  the  Manage¬ 
ment.  In  my  opinion,  in  the  special  circumstances  of  the 
case,  it  was  necessary  for  the  Enquiry  Officer  to  grant  the 
aforesaid  reques'  of  the  Workman  and  that  denial  of  Lawyer’s 
assistance  has  resulted  in  depriving  the  Workman  of  lenson- 
able  opportunity  to  defend  himself, 

8.  The  fuels  are  already  stated  in  detail  in  paras  3  and 
4  supra.  It  cannot  be  denied  that  the  allegations  made 
against  the  Woik.man,  if  duly  established  do  constitute  an 
offence  of  committing  forgery,  punishable  under  sections 
467/471  l.P.C,  For  proving  the  charges,  it  was  necessary 
for  the  Management  to  prove  that  the  School  J.eaving  Certi¬ 
ficate  which  showed  the  birth  date  of  the  Workman  as 
10-8-53,  was  a  forged  document  forged  cither  by  the  Work¬ 
man  himself  or  someone  else  for  him — and  wa.s  knowingly 
used  by  him  to  obtain  employment  with  the  Management. 
If  has  to  be  noted  that  the  allegation  that  the  Workman 
reprc.senled  1946  as  his  birth  year  while  applying  to  the 
Regional  Transport  Authority  for  license  for  driving  a  heavy 
vehicle,  does  not  ipso  facto  prove  that  his  real  birth  date  is 
19-8-46,  much  less  that  the  school  leaving  certificate  is 
forged  or  that  the  date  19-8-53  mentioned  in  the  school  re¬ 
cord  and  the  application  for  employment  to  the  Management 
is  false.  It  is  equally  probable  that  the  date  represented  to 
the  R.T.A.  may  be  incorrect  or  false,  the  motive  for  the 
Workman  being  to  pass  off  as  a  major  and  eligible  for  the 
licence.  What  I  mean  to  impress  is  that  the  facts  involved 
in  the  inquiry  were  complicated,  with  pretty  serious  conse¬ 
quences  for  the  Workman.  A  Lawyer's  assistance  was  also- 
lutely  necessary,  to  enable  the  Workman  to  effectively  defend 
himself. 

9.  The  prejudice  resultuig  from  the  denial  of  this  oppoi- 
tunity  t'j  the  Workman  is  '‘S'rit  large  on  the  enquiry  proceed¬ 
ings  themselves.  The  Enquiry  Officer  took  the  rather  unusual 
course  of  exhibiting  one  document  afler  the  other,  ns  ten¬ 
dered  hv  the  Presenting  Officer.  It  did  not  occur  to  him 
that  evidence  of  witnesses  concerned,  was  absolutely  neces¬ 
sary  not  only  to  enable  the  Workman  to  cross-examine  then, 
but  primarily  also  for  the  Management  themselves  to  estab¬ 
lish  their  serious  allegations  against  the  Workman.  The 


Enquiry  Ollicer  did  not  ask  any  questions  to  the  Workman 
on  the  several  documents,  which  he  placed  on  record,  with¬ 
out  obtaining  the  Workman’s  consent  or  examining  any  wit¬ 
nesses.  In.tially  he  declined  to  grant  copies  of  ceifain  docu¬ 
ments  to  the  Workman  on  the  imsustainabic  ground  that  the 
originals  ought  to  be  in  possession  of  the  latter.  Then  he 
iailed  to  icalise  that  it  was  not  enough  for  the  Workman 
to  get  copie.s  of  documents,  as  and  when  it  suited  the  con¬ 
venience  of  the  Management  to  produce  them.  Needless  to 
soy,  a  Workman — ^particularly  in  a  case  like  the  present  one, 
which  involved  complicated  allegations  with  serious  conse¬ 
quences — should  be  entitled  to  get  copies  of  all  documents 
(at  least  all  material  documents)  sought  to  be  relied  upon  by 
the  Management  in  advance  before  examination  of  witnesses 
starts,  so  that  he  can  have  a  conspectus  of  the  Management’s 
case  and  evidence  in  one  piece  and  effectively  and  meaning¬ 
fully  prepare  his  defence.  On  25-6-87,  it  appears  that  the 
Management  did  furnish  copies  of  the  several  documents 
produced  by  them  and  exhibited  by  the  Enquiry  Officer,  How¬ 
ever  the  Enquiry  Officer  failed  to  appreciate  that  it  would 
be  well-high  impossible  for  the  Workman,  even  with  the 
assistance  of  Guikwad — who  is  no  more  than  a  Clerk — to 
expla'n  as  many  as  ten  documents  and  adverse  circumstances. 
Indeed  the  situation  was  made  virtually  inractable  by  the 
“Enquiry  Officer’s  failure  to  put  each  document  to  the  Work¬ 
man  and  invite  his  explanation  on  adverse  circumstances,  i 
am  pointing  out  those  aspects,  not  to  blame  the  Enquiry 
Officer  as  such;  my  only  endeavour  is  to  stress  that  if  the 
Workman  bad  been  allowed  to  be  represented  by  a  Lawyer, 
the  enquiry  officer  also  would  have  got  the  benefit  of  useful 
and  right  advice  on  material  aspects— such  as  examining  wit¬ 
nesses  concerned  in  order  to  prove  the  documents  and  other 
missing  links,  if  any,  and  follow  the  principles  of  natural 
justice.  Much  more  important  thing  was  that  the  Workman 
would  have  been  properly  equipped  to  put  his  defence 
effectively, 

10.  The  legal  position  on  the  question  relating  to  a  Work¬ 
man’s  right  to  be  repre.sentcd  by  a  Lawyer  is  by  now  more 
or  less  well  settled.  As  observed  by  a  Division  Bench  of  the 
Bombay  High  Court  in  1986  fL.L.N.  743  A.B.  Eurtado  Vs 
Chairman  and  Managing  Director,  .Bank  of  India,  Bombay 
and  others  although  ordinarily  Courts  should  discourage  the 
involvement  of  legal  practitioners  in  domestic  inquiries  in 
order  to  avoid  delay  and  complexitie.s,  nevertheless,  it  cannot 
be  ignored  that  in  exceptional  cases,  representation  by  a 
lawyer  is  ncce.vsary,  for  otherwise  there  may  be  a  failure 
of  the  inquiry  itself  and  a  denial  of  a  proper  and  effective 
defence,  In  the  aforesaid  case  the  charge  against  the  Work¬ 
man  was  of  misappropriation  and  fraud  in  respect  of  Bank 
funds.  It  was  held  by  the  High  Court  that  the  charge  was 
serious  in  nature,  which,  if  proved,  may  invite  evil  conse¬ 
quences  for  the  Workman,  such  as  loss  of  reputation  and 
means  of  livelihood.  Lawyer’s  assistance  was  held  necessary, 
despite  the  fact  that  neither  the  Enquiry  Officer  nor  the 
Presenting  Officer  was  ,an  Advocate  or  otherwise  trained  in 
Law.  In  my  humble  opinion,  the  ratio  of  the  Furtado’s  case 
applies  to  the  fact  of  the  present  care  vi'ith  all  force. 

1 1.  Shri  Kele  for  the  Management  has  relied  upon  a  deci¬ 
sion  of  the  Bombay  High  Court  (Pendse  J.j  in  W.P.  No. 
1325/90  Bunoiigbs  Welcome  (India)  Ltd.  Vs.  V.  S.Guiao 
and  one  another  dated  11th  June,  1990,  for  the  preposition 
that  a  lawyer  cannot  be  permitted  to  represent  a  workman 
in  a  domestic  inquiry,  unless  the  standing  orders  applicable 
fo  him  enable  him  to.  The  case  is  clearly  distinguishable 
on  facta.  There  the  question  was  not  whether  a  Workman  is 
at  all  entitled  to  be  repre.scnted  by  a  Lawyer  under  any 
circumstances.  The  question  was  whether  a  Workman  had 
a  right  to  be  represented  by  any  person  of  his  cho-cc — in 
(he  case,  by  one  Carraniete- — and  the  Court  held  that  the 
Workman  had  no  .such  choice,  unless  granted  by  Standinir 
Orders.  There  is  nothing  in  the  iudgment  to  suggest  that  the 
Carrapictc  was  a  lawy'cr,  The  aforesaid  riilin.e  in  not  appli¬ 
cable  here.  If  at  all  any  authority  was  needed  for  the 
well  established  principle  th.nt  in  proper  and  special  cir¬ 
cumstances  the  workman  has  a  right  to  be  represented  by  a 
lawyer,  notwithstanding  that  the  relevant  Rules  do  not  pro¬ 
vide  for  surh  a  right,  reference  may  be  made  with  advanlage 
to  the  Simi'eme  Court  decision  renorted  in  1983  IL.L.N, 
314  Board  of  Tiustces  of  the  Port  of  Bomhav  and  a  Division 
Bench  decision  of  our  High  Court  in  1984  U  L.L.N.  294

factor  of  law’ver's  assistance.,

not  correct.  Issue  No.  6  is  therefore  found  in  the  negative. 
As  such  the  workman  is  not  entitled  to  any  relef.  Issue  No 
7  is  found  accordingly.

It  is  not  true  that  the  chargesheet  was 
issued  to  the  workman  because  he  was  an 
active  member  of  the  Union.  The  w'orkman 
i'.  entitled  to  full  wages  in  case  he  is  not 
found  guilty  of  the  charges  levelled  against 
him  as  provided  in  clause  33  of  the  Stand¬ 
ing  Orders,  In  the  present  case  the  work¬ 
man  was  found  guilty  of  the  charges  level¬ 
led  against  him  and  he  was  awarded  the 
punishment  of  reduction  of  one  increment. 
As  such  he  is  not  entitled  to  full  wages  of 
the  .suspension  period,  The  management 
therefore,  lastly  prayed  that  action  be  held 
as  just  and  proper  and  the  prayer  of  the 
Union  be  rejected.

industrial  Employment  (Standing  Orders)  Act,  1946. 
Ex.  11  is  a  copy  of  extract  of  the  1  st  AgreemenL 
dated  15-5-1955  between  the  Indian  Airline.s  Corpora¬

.Act  will  be  framed  by  the  Airhnes  Corpoartion.’  Thus, 
the  standing  orders  were  to  be  framed  under  the  In¬ 
dustrial  Employment  (Standing  Orders)  Act  by  the 
Corporation.  Ex.  12  is  a  copy  of  the  Award  of  the 
National  Industrial  ilribunal,  Lucknow  of  the  year 
1958.  As  per  para,  241  (h)  (i)  there  to  alterations 
or  modifications  of  service  rules  or  Standing  Orders 
will  be  done  in  accordance  with  the  procedure  laid 
down  in  the  Industrial  Employment  (Standing  Orders) 
Ac:  or  under  mutual  agreement.  Ex,  13  is  a  copy  of 
extract  of  written  statement  of  Indian  Airlines  in  Re¬ 
ference  Order  No.  SRO  dated  9-11-1957  pending 
before  the  National  Industrial  Tribunal  at  Lucknow. 
In  that  written  statement  the  Indian  Airlines  Corpo¬ 
ration  had  made  a  statement  that  “in  compliance 
with  the  agreement,  standing  orders  have  been  fram¬ 
ed  by  the  Corporation  and  copies  sent  to  the  various 
State  Labour  Cojnmi.ssioners  for  certification.  How¬ 
ever,  the  Union  b.as  placed  ob.stacles  in  tlie  way  of

agreements”.  Thus.  Indian  Airlines  Corporation  ad¬ 
mitted  that  the  standing  orders  were  framed  by  them

Act.  However,  the  Corporation  further  stated  in  that 
written  statement  thus  : — 

“In  accordance  with  the  agrecjiient  dated 
1-5-1955,  standing  orders  were  submitted 
for  certification  under  the  Tmlustrial  Em¬ 
ployment  (Standing  Orders)  Act,  but  these 
have  not  yet  been  certified  due  to  hindranc¬ 
es  placed  by  Ibc  Union.  In  the  meantime 
the  Corporation  is  fully  authorised  lo  fcrime 
Standing  Orders  in  accordance  with  the 
procedure  laid  down  under  the  Air  Corpo¬ 
ration  Act.” 

(Thus  the  Air  Corporation  made  it  quite  dear  in  that 
written  statement  that  the  Standing  Orders  framed 
under  the  Industrial  Employment  (Staxiding  Orders) 
Act  could  not  be  brought  into  operation  due  to  the 
hindrancc.s  caused  by  the  Union,  and  that  they  had 
framed  Standing  Orders  under  the  Air  Corporation 
Aci.  Therefore,  as  the  standing  orders  framed  under 
the  Industrid  Ec^loyment  (Standing  Orders)  Act, 
sent  for  certification  by  the  various  Labour  Commis¬ 
sioner  are  pending,  they  do  not  and  did  not  get  any 
legal  force,  and  as  such,  the  standing  orders  framed 
bv  the  Corporation  under  the  Air  Corporation  Act 
will  prevail  over  the  Standing  Orders  contemp'ated 
under  the  Industrial  Employment  (Standing  Orders) 
Act.  Therefore,  there  was  no  question  of  compliance 
with  the  model  standing  orders  as  per  the  prov’sons 
of  the  Industrial  Emplovmcnt  (Standing  Orders)  AcI, 
as  contended  by  the  Union.  Issue  No,  3  is  found 
accbrdingly,

ISSUES  NOS.  6  and  7 

14.  According  to  the  Union  the  management 
should  have  paid  full  wages  to  the  workman  of  the 
period  of  suspension.  However,  as  the  Standing 
Orders  framed  under  the  Air  Corporation  Act,  and 
noi  the  model  Standing  Orders  framed  under  the 
Industrial  Employment  (Standing  Orders)  Act,  apply 
to  the  management  and  the  workmen,  the  workman 
IS  entitled  to  full  wage.s  in  case  he  is  not  found  guilty 
of  the  charges  levelled  against  him.  In  the  present 
case  the  charges  have  been  duly  proved,  iThcrefore, 
the  workman  is  not  entitled  to  full  wa>ies.  but  is  en¬ 
titled  only  to  112  the  wages  of  the  period  of  suspen¬ 
sion.  Therefore,  the  allegation  of  the  Union  in  the 
matter  is  not  correct.  Issue  No.  6  is  found  in  the  ne¬ 
gative.  .As  such  the  workman  is  entitled  to  no  relief 
in  that  re  pect,

10.  Issues  .No.  1  to  3  : — 1  will  take  up  all  these 
issues  together.  It  is  true  Onit  it  has  been  challen¬

workman  concerned  to  prove  that  he  is  a  w'orkman. 
Without  going  into  the  details  of  these  facts  which 
were  not  hotly  contested  it  can  be  safely  said  that 
the  workman  wai  workman  within,  the  delinifioii  of 
Sec.  2(s)  of  the  1.  D.  Act.  These  facts  can  be  well 
gathered  from  the  documents  Ex.  Mil  and  Ex.  Ml 2, 
his  attendance  register  Ex.  M|3  to  Ex.  M'29  as  also 
Ex.  Ml 30  with  annexur.e  Ikd  of  the  candidates.  Ex. 
Ml 31  is  .Agreement  between  the  management  and  the 
employees  Federation,  according  to  which  the  work¬ 
men  who  had  completed  one  year  service  should  be 
absorbed.  Ex.  M!32  is  another  letter. 

11.  Management  has  examined  R.  L.  Agarwal 
Officer  of  the  Ba.nk  in  support  of  its  case  while  the 
workman  examined  none. 

12.  According  to  this  witness  the  workman  had 
joined  at  Seont  Branch,  State  Bank  of  India,  as  tem¬ 
porary  Cashier-cum-Clerk  011  15-11-1965.  He  was 
appointed  against  leave  vacancy  of  S,  K.  Jain  and 
was  given  an  appointment  ordc^r.  He  brought  the 
register  for  the  per'od  from  15-11-65  to  31-12-66. 
Acceding  to  him,  workman  worked  upto  31-12-66 
and  his  services  were  terminated  with  effect  from 
1-1-1967. 

13.  Taking  the  case  as  it  is.  it  is  obvious  that  the 
management  has  not  counted  his  service  prior  to  the 
period  1-1-1966.  According  to  the  workman,  he  un- 
disputedly  served  from  15-11-1965  and  this  fact  has 
been  admitted  by  tlie  management. 

14.  As  I  hove  already  pointed  out  above,  manage¬ 
ment  has  deniv  ci  th>at  the  workman  concerned  had. 
worked  from  8-11-1965  to  31-12-1965  i.e.  for  a 
period  of  54  days  it  follow,'  that  the  management  has 
nothing  to  say  against  it.  In  its  various  statements 
and  rejoinders  they  have  given  details  of  dates  during 
which  this  workman  had  worked  in  the  year  1966. 
Even  M.W.  I,  R.  L.  Agarwal,  has  partly  admitted 
the  case  of  the  workman  inasmuch  as  he  admitted 
that  this  workman  joined  the  State  Bank  of  India  on 
15-11-1965.  The  management  has,  however,  filed 
the  attendance  register  of  the  workman  since 
20-12-1966  onwards  and  not  from  8-11-1965  Thus 
them  is  nothing  to  disbelieve  this  part  of  the  version 
of  the  workman  that  he  had  workeo  from  8-11-1965 
to  31-12-1965  in  the  year  1965.  It  follow.;  that  he 
worked  for  54  davs  upto  31-12-1965.  Thus  if  this 
period  is  added  to  the  period  of  working  days  of  this 
workman  as  given  by  the  management  i.e.  198  days, 
it  would  exceed  240  days.  But  the  question  is  whe¬ 
ther  this  period  of  54  days  should  also  be  computed 
for  the  purposes  of  computin-?  under  the  provisions 
of  Sec.  25B  read  with  Sec.  25F  of  the  I.D.  Act,  for 
this  period  of  service  of  the  workman  has;  been  com¬ 
pletely  ignored  by  the  management  and  according  to 
the  management  the  year  commences  from  1st  day 
of  each  English  Calendar  month  and  ends  with  last 
last  day  of  (he  English  Calendar  month.

In  a  concurring  judgment  Palhak  J.  agreed  with  this

be  deemed  to  be  'n  continuous  service  for  a  period 
of  one  year  for  the  purpo.se  of  Section  2.5B  and 
Chapter' VA.”

19.  Next  quc.slion  which  is  material  to  decide  in 
this  case  is  whether  the  service  rendered  by  the  work¬ 
man  for  a  period  of  240  days  was  continuous  service.

POINT  NO.  2

punishment  awarded  cannot  be  called  as  dispropor¬ 
tionate  or  excessive.  From  the  rules  governrng  the 
grant  of  housing  loans  it  appears  that  the  object  of 
the  scheme  is  lo  assist  an  employee  who  docs  not 
own  any  residential  house.  It  further  appears  that 
the  loan  for  ho’jse  granted  at  a  comparatively  low 
interest.  The  rates  of  interesi  are  given  at  page  3  of 
the  Rules,  Interest  at  commercial  rate  is  certainly 
much  higher.  The  purpose  will  st^d  defeated  if  in 
the  garb  of  acquiring  the  residential  house  with  the 
help  of  housing  loan  an  employee  tries  to  run  a  trade 
or  business  in  it  either  himself  or  through  one  or 
some  of  the  members  of  his  family.  On  point  No.  2 
also  the  Union  [workman  files.
